## **NATIONAL INSTITUTE FOR HEALTH AND CLINICAL EXCELLENCE** 

## **Health Technology Appraisal** 

**Dasatinib, high-dose imatinib and nilotinib for the treatment of imatinib-resistant chronic myeloid leukaemia (CML) (part review of NICE technology appraisal guidance 70), and dasatinib and nilotinib for people with CML for whom treatment with imatinib has failed because of intolerance Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the Appraisal Consultation Document (ACD)** 

## **Definitions:** 

**Consultees –** Organisations that accept an invitation to participate in the appraisal including the manufacturer or sponsor of the technology, national professional organisations, national patient organisations, the Department of Health and the Welsh Assembly Government and relevant NHS organisations in England. Consultee organisations are invited to submit evidence and/or statements and respond to consultations. They are also have right to appeal against the Final Appraisal Determination (FAD). Consultee organisations representing patients/carers and professionals can nominate clinical specialists and patient experts to present their personal views to the Appraisal Committee. 

**Clinical specialists and patient experts** – Nominated specialists/experts have the opportunity to make comments on the ACD separately from the organisations that nominated them. They do not have the right of appeal against the FAD other than through the nominating organisation. 

**Commentators –** Organisations that engage in the appraisal process but that are not asked to prepare an evidence submission or statement. They are invited to respond to consultations but, unlike consultees, they do not have the right of appeal against the FAD. These organisations include manufacturers of comparator technologies, NHS Quality Improvement Scotland, the relevant National Collaborating Centre (a group commissioned by the Institute to develop clinical guidelines), other related research groups where appropriate (for example, the Medical Research Council and National Cancer Research Institute); other groups (for example, the NHS Confederation, NHS Information Authority and NHS Purchasing and Supplies Agency, and the _British National Formulary_ ). **Public –** Members of the public have the opportunity to comment on the ACD when it is posted on the Institute‟s web site 5 days after it is sent to consultees and commentators. These comments are usually presented to the appraisal committee in full, but may be summarised by the Institute secretariat – for example when many letters, emails and web site comments are received and recurring themes can be identified.

## **Comments received from consultees** 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|Royal<br>College of<br>Pathologists<br>(RCPath)|Unfortunately, this is not good news for CML patients who are currently receiving imatinib at<br>the standard dose (400 mg daily), nor for future patients yet to be diagnosed. This decision<br>denies access to the second generation agents dasatinib and nilotinib, for patients whose<br>leukaemia becomes resistant to imatinib. This is approximately 40% of CML patients, which is<br>about 300 patients per annum across the UK.<br>We have very recently been carrying out a population study of CML outcome in the<br>geographically contiguous area of Merseyside, Cheshire, Isle of Man and the North Wales<br>coastal strip (total population = 2.0 million). During the 7 year period 2003-2009, 192 patients<br>were newly diagnosed, of whom 9 presented with advanced disease, 3 did not receive a<br>tyrosine kinase inhibitor (TKI) because of extreme age, and 20 received a second generation<br>TKI as first line treatment as part of a clinical trial. A total of 160 therefore received first line<br>imatinib, and 123 are assessable at 36 months, of which 69 (56%) have achieved adequate<br>responses (at least complete cytogenetic remission). Thirty-three patients (which are approx.<br>40% of cases diagnosed since second generation TKI became locally available in Jan 2006)<br>required switching to a second generation TKI because of imatinib resistance (18 cases) or<br>intolerance (15 cases). Of these, 21 of 30 (70%) assessable cases have achieved not only<br>complete cytogenetic remission but major molecular response, and no patient has lost this<br>response at latest follow-up.<br>It is known from the IRIS study that the progression rate to advanced disease for patients<br>unable to achieve complete cytogenetic response on imatinib is substantial, of the order of<br>10% over the subsequent 3-5 years. However, the progression rate for patients in major<br>molecular response is less than 1%. In this population study, second generation TKI have<br>therefore converted 70% of imatinib resistant/intolerant patients from a progression risk of<br>10% to <1%. It is too soon to detect an effect on progression free survival (though a trend is<br>currently apparent), but the overall rate of complete cytogenetic response is superior for<br>patients diagnosed since 1/1/2006 (p = 0.04), which is when second generation TKI became<br>locally available for imatinib resistance/intolerance.<br>In summary, these very recent population data provide evidence that second generation TKI<br>benefit 70% of the 40% of CML patients in whom they are indicated.|The Committee concluded that it is clear that<br>dasatinib, high-dose imatinib and nilotinib<br>provide clinical benefit for people with imatinib-<br>resistant CML. However, the Committee agreed<br>that the limited evidence base means that the<br>magnitude of the benefit is uncertain. See FAD<br>section 4.3.9|

|RCPath|There are problems with all of the 4 health economic models used in the NICE assessment. In<br>addition, each of these has used the wrong comparator; either hydroxycarbamide, interferon<br>or stem cell transplantation. In earlier meetings and documentation (and in the response from<br>this college to the first appraisal meeting), it was recommended that the appropriate<br>comparator should be high-dose imatinib. This has however been included as an appraisal<br>technology instead. In this case, assuming that high-dose imatinib were also unavailable, then<br>the relevant comparator should be maintaining the patients on standard dose imatinib. A<br>detailed discussion of these points is given in the attached response to NICE from the NCRI<br>CML subgroup (attached).|The clinical specialists suggested that if<br>dasatinib, high-dose imatinib or nilotinib were not<br>available, people would receive treatment with<br>interferon alfa, hydroxycarbamide or best<br>supportive care. The Committee also heard from<br>the clinical specialists that bone marrow stem<br>cell transplantation could be used. The<br>Committee concluded that any one of these<br>treatments could be considered a comparator<br>with high-dose imatinib, nilotinib or dasatinib.<br>See FAD section 4.3.3|
|RCPath|The appraisal committee has acknowledged that the appraisal technologies provide clinical<br>benefit in imatinib-resistant CML (paragraph 4.3.6). We of course agree with this. It is<br>therefore plausible that these technologies (especially dasatinib and nilotinib) may well<br>achieve an ICER below the £30,000 threshold, if their NHS price were reduced. Novartis have<br>recently dropped the effective NHS price of nilotinib for first line use (at a dose of 300mg twice<br>daily) to match that of imatinib, whilst leaving the price of the licensed dose for second line<br>use (400mg twice daily) unaltered.  If there were downward movement of the price also for<br>second line use, and a similar move from Bristol Myers Squibb for dasatinib, these drugs may<br>then achieve the NICE model of cost-effectiveness. It is however difficult for the College or for<br>individual clinicians to take a view on the detail of this; discussion between the manufacturers<br>and the Department of Health are therefore required. One suggestion is that this could be<br>pegged to the establishment of a CML registry across the UK, as already in place for the<br>North of England and Wales; the UK clinical CML community would welcome and support this.|The Committee noted that the manufacturer of<br>nilotinib had agreed a patient access scheme<br>with the Department of Health. The Committee<br>recommended the use of nilotinib for the<br>treatment of adults with chronic and accelerated<br>phase CML that is resistant to standard-dose<br>imatinib or who have imatinib intolerance, if the<br>manufacturer makes nilotinib available with the<br>discount agreed as part of the patient access<br>scheme. See FAD section 4.3.21–4.3.23<br>The Committee noted that, given the patient<br>access scheme for nilotinib and the assumed<br>equivalence of effectiveness of dasatinib and<br>nilotinib, dasatinib is considerably more<br>expensive but no more effective than nilotinib.<br>See FAD section 4.3.25|
|Royal<br>College of<br>Nursing<br>(RCN)|The evidence considered seems comprehensive.|Comment noted|

|RCP|The analysis considered four different models, all of which are considered flawed by our<br>experts. That from Bristol Myers Squibb assumes that chronic phase patients can only<br>progress; there is no possibility of progressing patients being able to achieve a second chronic<br>phase (which is achievable clinically in approximately 30% of cases). This model uses 5<br>„disease states‟ linked to prognosis; these do not reflect real clinical practice, as they assume<br>that once a response is achieved, it does not change until disease progression. For example,<br>it does not encompass slow responding patients who may take time to move from no<br>response to cytogenetic response and then molecular response. The treatment options at<br>imatinib failure were obtained from a postal survey, but it is not clear whether continuance of<br>standard dose imatinib was presented as an option if none of the appraisal technologies were<br>available. Interferon was used as the comparator, apparently using first-line efficacy data from<br>the 1990s. Apart from being an inappropriate comparator, these data are likely to be<br>overoptimistic when applied to imatinib resistant patients, who are „selected‟ for having<br>unfavourable disease. Interestingly, the costs of nilotinib and high-dose imatinib in this model<br>are more than double that in the other models.|The Committee considered that the model<br>developed by Bristol-Myers Squibb had a<br>number of limitations, of which the most<br>important were that it estimated the cost for<br>people receiving interferon alfa to be higher than<br>(in some cases double) that of all the other<br>economic models, and it did not include a<br>comparison with hydroxycarbamide. |RCP|The model from Novartis compared nilotinib and high dose imatinib against the comparators<br>stem cell transplantation (SCT) and hydroxycarbamide. Apart from being inappropriate<br>comparators, this model also assumed that CML phases are only consecutive (ie that second<br>chronic phase is not possible). The assumption that 75% of patients under 65 can undergo<br>allogeneic SCT, and that all other patients receive hydroxycarbamide is considered<br>unrealistic.|The Committee considered the economic model<br>for chronic-phase CML that is resistant to<br>standard-dose imatinib in the model developed<br>by Novartis. See FAD section<br>4.3.22|

|RCP|That from PENTAG uses a model in which chronic phase patients have only 2 states; those<br>with a major cytogenetic response and those without. Interferon is<br>again used as a comparator, to derive ICERs that are in each case well in excess of a<br>£30,000 threshold. However, our experts believe that the interferon data appear highly<br>unusual (treatment for 2.04 years with interferon is associated with 10.75 years of survival;<br>paragraph 4.2.19). This analysis also predicts overall survival of 12.98 and 13.4 years of<br>survival with nilotinib and dasatinib respectively, despite the fact that neither drug was<br>available in any trial until 2004/5, so long term survival is unknown. It also does not take<br>account of the clinical observation that responses to dasatinib/nilotinib vary from minimal right<br>through to complete molecular remissions, which are likely to have very different resultant<br>survivals.|The Committee noted the PenTAG model did not<br>link treatment duration with overall survival and<br>that some of the results were not plausible. |RCP|The final model is from SHTAC, and addresses some of the deficiencies in the other models<br>(eg the PENTAG assumption that treatment duration is twice as long for nilotinib as for<br>dasatinib). No allowance is made for varying depths of response, in which long<br>term outcome varies widely. It appears that at a threshold of £30,000 per QALY<br>gained, nilotinib and dasatinib have probabilities of being cost effective of 60% and 28%<br>respectively.|The Committee noted the PenTAG model did not<br>link treatment duration with overall survival and<br>that some of the results were not plausible. It<br>understood that the SHTAC base-case treatment<br>durations still did not reflect the fact that in<br>clinical practice, people will receive treatment<br>until progression or death. See FAD section<br>4.3.17|

|---|---|---|
|RCP|Our experts believe that the wrong comparator has been used in each of the models. If none<br>of the appraisal technologies were available, it is highly likely that the majority of patients<br>would simply remain on standard dose imatinib. The statement that hydroxycarbamide is<br>associated with a median survival of 5 years in the setting of imatinib resistance (paragraph<br>4.3.3) is not based on any data and appears extremely overoptimistic. We note that the issue<br>of comparators is incorrectly summarised at point 4.3.2. We are aware that in the written<br>evidence to the committee from the Royal College of Pathologists, it is stated that if neither<br>dasatinib nor nilotinib were available, then most clinicians would opt for high-dose imatinib.<br>However, hitherto the question had not arisen of what to do if high-dose imatinib were also not<br>available. In this case, we believe that most clinicians would advise remaining on standard<br>dose imatinib. We understand that this was also stated at the appraisal meeting.<br>There are several reasons why continuing standard dose imatinib in imatinib resistance may<br>be reasonable:<br>a) In the majority, haematological response is maintained. Most would be reluctant to change<br>to therapies that need to be given by injection and associated with many side effects<br>(interferon) or risk cytogenetic deterioration (hydroxycarbamide).<br>b) There is laboratory evidence to support the notion that disease progression may<br>commonly arise in a mature progenitor cell compartment, rather than the leukaemic stem<br>cell. Whilst imatinib does not target the latter, it does reduce the progenitor cell<br>compartment, unlike hydroxycarbamide. This observation may explain the paradox that<br>imatinib decreases progression rates whilst being ineffective against leukaemic stem cells.<br>The only other treatment that is logical is stem cell transplantation (SCT). However, this is only<br>feasible for fewer than 30% of patients, because of age and donor availability. Even if feasible,<br>SCT is associated with significant permanent morbidity and some mortality, and some patients<br>will opt to wait until clear evidence of disease progression before accepting SCT|The clinical specialists suggested that if<br>dasatinib, high-dose imatinib or nilotinib were not<br>available, people would receive treatment with<br>interferon alfa, hydroxycarbamide or best<br>supportive care. The Committee also heard from<br>the clinical specialists that bone marrow stem<br>cell transplantation could be used. The<br>Committee concluded that any one of these<br>treatments could be considered a comparator<br>with high-dose imatinib, nilotinib or dasatinib..<br>See FAD section 4.3.3|
|RCP|Once patients progress to blast crisis, the only option likely to achieve long term good health<br>is SCT. This is typically preceded by acute leukaemia style chemotherapy (usually 2 courses)<br>with a concurrent second generation TKI, rather than the TKI alone. Unfortunately, SCT in this<br>situation has a mortality approaching 50% (partly due to relapse), and is in any case only<br>feasible in about 30% of patients, as above. The outlook for those who cannot receive SCT is<br>grave, with a median survival of less than 24 months; those who are too unfit to withstand<br>acute leukaemia treatment fare particularly badly. Only dasatinib has a product licence for<br>blast crisis, but clinical experience suggests that it may palliate the unpleasant symptoms of<br>blast crisis for many months, ensuring reasonable quality of life for the majority of patients.<br>This is especially important for the considerable number of patients who cannot undergo acute<br>leukaemia-style treatment. It appears that blast crisis may therefore meet NICE criteria for<br>„end of life‟, and indeed this point was made at the April appraisal committee meeting.|The Committee heard from the clinical<br>specialists that treatment strategy in the blast-<br>crisis phase of the disease is different from that<br>in the accelerated or chronic phases, with<br>dasatinib and high-dose imatinib given as<br>adjuvant treatment with intensive chemotherapy<br>for acute leukaemia. See FAD section 4.3.12<br>and 4.3.27|

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|BMS|There is no question that, for patients who are imatinib-resistant, both dasatinib and nilotinib<br>are transformational treatments. They may offer a real chance of full life expectancy for a<br>significant group of patients who would otherwise have reached the end of the road with<br>regard to their treatment options, other than a bone marrow transplant. This last point appears<br>to be accepted by all.<br>The AC acknowledges also that, even in the advanced stages of the disease, there is likely to<br>be real clinical benefit and that there is merit in the view of clinicians that patients in a real-life<br>setting are likely to perform better than those in trials. This is because they will access<br>treatment earlier and are likely to be in better overall physical state than trial patients. Whilst<br>accepting that this benefit cannot be accurately quantified, and given the impossibility of<br>generating new comparative data (what we have is realistically all that can ever - ethically - be<br>generated), this is surely a situation where the potential benefits of the improved real-life<br>performance and the clinical support for these benefits should be given greater weight than<br>the uncertainty. This is doubly the case, given that any effective alternative for patients at this<br>stage is likely to be associated with a substantially sub-optimal side-effect profile.<br>We have noted below the areas where we feel that the model has significant shortcomings,<br>which have the effect of increasing of the ICER. Correction of these would offset many of the<br>increases in the ICERs that the AC believes result from assuming a longer duration of<br>treatment, and would provide more overall certainty. Our modelling leads us to believe that it<br>should be possible to end up with ICER values that, with greater certainty, are in the normal<br>range considered acceptable under the end of life criteria|Comment noted.|



1. Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 11 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|BMS|Our main points of contention with the ACD are as follows:<br>The original (and revised) Assessment Group (AG) economic model contains fundamental<br>shortcomings and cannot be reliably used for decision-making. These relate both to the<br>assumptions on which the model is built and the methods used in its construction. The<br>model allows for individuals to spend longer in one health state than they do alive, and uses<br>inconsistent approaches to modelling key parameters for different drugs. The approach also<br>allows for individuals on the older, less effective interventions to have a lower rate of<br>disease progression than those on newer and more effective drugs.<br>The interpretation of „continuation of treatment until death‟ is flawed.  Whilst we accept that<br>treatments are given until death for patients who continue to respond to treatment,<br>however, importantly, they are also given until disease progression, or until the<br>development of intolerance. It is incorrect to assume that the same treatment is always<br>given until death.<br>The AC‟s interpretation of methods and outputs of BMS modelling is incorrect, as discussed<br>in detail in section 2.3. This results in conclusions that are unfair and unrepresentative of<br>the true value of these technologies.<br>Additional evidence does, in fact, exist beyond that considered by the AC. For example, in<br>order to make an informed decision around the comparative cost effectiveness of<br>hydroxycarbamide (on the assumption that the AC insists it is “...probably the most<br>appropriate comparator”, a position with which BMS and clinical experts disagree), an in-<br>depth analysis of the efficacy and cost of this treatment, should surely be required from the<br>AG and manufacturers. The current conclusion about hydroxycarbamide is based on the<br>contested AG model.<br>„face validity‟ check of model outputs represents an over-simplification of the real-life clinical<br>situation. The AC has disregarded the overall treatment costs that should include much<br>more expensive and more complicated post-failure treatments such as Bone Marrow Stem<br>Cell Transplantation (BMSCT), (predominately a 3rd line therapy). If, as implied by the<br>ACD, dasatinib and nilotinib were not available more patients would need to receive these<br>high cost 3rd line treatments at increased cost to the NHS. Such an outcome seems<br>counter-intuitive. The practical reality of this is, that in the absence of TKIs in the 2nd line<br>setting at a rough estimate a minimum of 300 patients each year will be left with a BMSCT<br>as their only realistic treatment option. This is likely to stretch the bone marrow transplant<br>infrastructure in England and Wales to breaking point and to impose significant extra costs<br>onto the NHS.<br>Where BMSCT is the only credible treatment option it is likely to exclude many patients who<br>are not Caucasian, young and healthy. This is due to the lack of suitable donors, so that the<br>effect of this guidance is to discriminate against anyone who is of an ethnic minority, old<br>andwith multiple co-morbidities.|The Committee noted the PenTAG model did not<br>link treatment duration with overall survival and<br>that some of the results were not plausible. It<br>understood that the SHTAC base-case treatment<br>durations still did not reflect the fact that in<br>clinical practice, people will receive treatment<br>until progression or death. See FAD sections<br>4.3.17 and 4.3.18.<br>The Committee considered all the evidence<br>submitted, including evidence from clinical trials,<br>patient and clinical experts, the Assessment<br>Group‟s economic analysis and the<br>manufacturers‟ submissions. It also carefully<br>considered the comments received from<br>consultees and commentators in response to the<br>Assessment Report.<br>The reliability of the available evidence is also<br>considered by the Committee when formulating<br>its recommendations.<br>The Committee noted the argument that if<br>dasatinib, high-dose imatinib or nilotinib are not<br>recommended for the treatment of imatinib-<br>resistant CML and that this could raise issues in<br>relation to race, age (the elderly), and<br>comorbidities. However, the Committee<br>concluded that allowing for clinical decisions<br>relating to a range of possible treatments based<br>on individual assessment of risk and benefit<br>does not limit access to the technology for any<br>specific protected group compared with other<br>people. See FAD section 4.3.31|

The preliminary negative decision also<br>raises fundamental ethical and human right issues in that it prevents doctors from prescribing,<br>and patients from having access to, potentially life-saving treatment. 1. Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 13 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|BMS|_Paragraph 4.1.5: „PenTAG provided pooled summary results for three outcomes. A complete_<br>_cytogenetic response was reported in 37.4% of participants (95% confidence interval [CI] 34.2_<br>_to 40.5), 50.9% had a major cytogenetic response (95% CI 47.6 to 54.1) and 89.2% had a_<br>_complete haematological response (95% CI 87.2 to 91.3)‟._<br>**Comments**: Pooled complete cytogenic response (CCyR) (37.4%) is reported in PenTAG<br>report (Table 15) that includes 6-month follow-up data from BMS-034 (33.9%). The 24-month<br>follow-up data of this study shows a much higher CCyR (50%) (Shah et al 2010), and this has<br>not been taken account. There are similar discrepancies regarding the pooled major<br>cytogenetic response (MCyR) and complete haematological response (CHR) (see PenTAG<br>report: Table 16 and Table 25).|Comment noted. This section has been<br>amended (accordingly, for clarity).|
|BMS|_Paragraph 4.1.16: „One dose-ranging RCT and one single-arm cohort study were identified_<br>_that reported results for dasatinib in participants with accelerated-phase CML. The_<br>_Assessment Groups considered that the RCT was of low methodological quality as it_**_did not_**<br>**_report allocation concealment_**_and had an open-label design.‟_<br>**Comments**: The highlighted statement has not taken into account the evidence of the<br>allocation concealment reported in the clinical study report (CSR). The allocation concealment<br>is reported as: Each site enrolled subjects into the study at the time of eligibility screening by<br>accessing a call-in interactive voice response system (IVRS) after informed consent had been<br>obtained. A subject number was assigned at this time. After completion of all screening<br>evaluations, all eligible subjects were then randomly assigned to one of two treatment arms<br>(dasatinib or imatinib) in a 2:1 ratio. To enrol a subject, a phone call was made by the site to<br>the central randomization centre using a 24-hour toll-free number. The randomisation<br>procedure dynamically minimized the imbalance between treatment arms within each of the<br>following stratification factors: 1) site and 2) cytogenetic response on imatinib (cytogenetic<br>response (minimal, minor, partial or complete) vs no cytogenetic response). Upon completion<br>of randomisation, the first dose of study therapy was administered within three days.|Comment noted. The FAD has been amended to<br>reflect that this was a consideration of the<br>Assessment Groups - see FAD section 4.1.18|

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|BMS|_Paragraph 4.3.3: „The Committee heard from the clinical specialists that people whose CML_<br>_does not respond to dasatinib or nilotinib within 12 months_**_would receive treatment with_**<br>**_hydroxycarbamide_**_or, if suitable, stem cell transplantation. For people receiving_<br>_hydroxycarbamide, the prognosis is poor,_**_with a median life expectancy of around 5_**<br>**_years’_**_._<br>**Comments**: The clinical evidence, and clinical experts‟ opinions, have clearly been<br>misinterpreted. All the extensive, senior clinical advice that BMS has received and that NICE<br>has received as part of this appraisal (e.g. see comments from Dr Patrick Cadigan, RCP<br>Registrar on behalf of: NCRI/RCP/RCR/ACP/JCCO) clearly show that patients who do not<br>respond to dasatinib or nilotinib within 12 months, would NOT receive hydroxycarbamide<br>(considered obsolete in this indication). Instead they would receive another 2nd generation<br>TKI, and then proceed to BMSCT.<br>Historically, hydroxycarbamide has only been used in the 1st line setting. The evidence<br>relating to hydroxycarbamide in the 2nd line setting is minimal. It is safe to assume however<br>that patients resistant to 2nd generation TKIs are unlikely to respond to hydroxycarbamide<br>and the median survival is likely to be significantly less than the 5 years cited above (in the 1st<br>line setting). In this respect, the Chronic Myeloid Leukemia Trialists‟ Collaborative Group<br>(1997) state that, in newly diagnosed patients, the survival rate is only 44% at 5-years.|The clinical specialists suggested that if<br>dasatinib, high-dose imatinib or nilotinib were not<br>available, people would receive treatment with<br>interferon alfa, hydroxycarbamide or best<br>supportive care. The Committee also heard from<br>the clinical specialists that bone marrow stem<br>cell transplantation could be used. The<br>Committee concluded that any one of these<br>treatments could be considered a comparator<br>with high-dose imatinib, nilotinib or dasatinib.<br>See FAD section 4.3.3.|
|BMS|_Paragraph 4.3.4: „The Committee noted that the economic models available to them had used_<br>_major cytogenetic response as a surrogate for overall survival and heard from the clinical_<br>_specialists that_**_the strongest link was between major molecular response and overall_**<br>**_survival_**_.‟_<br>**Comments**: The highlighted statement is not a reflection of current clinical knowledge. As<br>noted in the ELN guideline (Baccarani et al 2009) and Marin et al (2008), complete<br>cytogenetic response is the most important response-related prognostic factor since Marin‟s et<br>al (2008) concluded that „At 12 months, the only independent predictors for PFS were: (1)<br>being in CCyR (complete cytogenetic response)...... and (2) prior loss of CCyR ……; At 18<br>months, the only independent predictor for PFS was being in CCyR‟. Furthermore, Marin et al<br>(2008) stated that „the achievement of MMR (major molecular response) at 12 or 18 months<br>**failed to**confer any benefit in 5-year PFS or OS‟.<br>The ELN guideline stated that: „In these patients (treated with dasatinib and nilotinib), the<br>response-related prognostic factors that have been identified for imatinib may apply as well to<br>dasatinib and nilotinib, but it should not be overlooked that the response to these drugs is<br>more rapid‟ (Baccarani et al 2009).|The Committee considered all the evidence<br>submitted, including evidence from clinical trials,<br>patient and clinical experts, the Assessment<br>Group‟s economic analysis and the<br>manufacturers‟ submissions. It also carefully<br>considered the comments received from<br>consultees and commentators in response to the<br>Assessment Report.|

|BMS|_Paragraph 4.3.4: „The Committee noted the_**_poor evidence base_**_for all interventions for_<br>_people whose CML is resistant to standard-dose imatinib‟_<br>**Comments**: This statement is not a reasonable interpretation of the evidence. There is<br>sufficient evidence demonstrating the safety and efficacy of dasatinib from 7 phase 2 and<br>phase 3 clinical trials that include over 2,000 patients. Whilst we appreciate the issue<br>regarding the paucity of comparative data, the majority of the available evidence supports<br>dasatinib as an effective intervention. Such trials were considered sufficiently robust for the<br>European Medicines Agency to grant a marketing authorisation and therefore we fail to see<br>how the AC can consider the evidence base poor.|This information was included in the ACD, but<br>has been amended following comments from<br>other consultees.|
|BMS|_Paragraph 4.3.5: „The Committee was aware that no evidence was presented on the use of_<br>_dasatinib, high-dose imatinib and nilotinib (accelerated phase only) in this way (i.e._**_as_**<br>**_adjuvant treatment with intensive chemotherapy for acute leukaemia_**_) and that the_<br>_evidence base in this advanced stage of the disease was very limited‟._<br>**Comments**: This statement is not a reasonable interpretation of the evidence. In advanced<br>phase CML there is evidence regarding the clinical efficacy of dasatinib monotherapy<br>((Apperley et al 2009, Kantarjian et al 2009, Cortes et al 2008). It should be remembered that<br>this appraisal is to assess the clinical and cost-effectiveness of dasatinib used in CML patients<br>as monotherapy (as defined in the final scope). It is not to assess dasatinib used in the neo-<br>adjuvant/adjuvant setting (TKI initiated before and/or after BMSCT) and any such discussions<br>or opinions in this ACD are procedurally unfair.|See FAD 4.3.12 and 4.3.27 – It has been<br>clarified that this comment relates to the blast<br>phase crisis only. The Committee noted that<br>usual treatment for the blast-crisis phase is<br>different from that used in the other phases.|



1. Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 16 -->

## Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|BMS|_Paragraph 4.3.8: „The Committee was aware that,_**_for people who respond, these_**<br>**_treatments (dasatinib and nilotinib) are given daily, very often until death_**_, and that this_<br>_results in high treatment costs for every year of life lived with CML‟_<br>**Comments**: This statement is not a reasonable interpretation of the evidence and<br>oversimplifies the clinical situation. The interpretation of „continuation of treatment until death‟<br>is flawed.  Whilst we accept that treatments are given until death for patients who continue to<br>respond to treatment, however, importantly, they are also given until disease progression, or<br>until the development of intolerance. It is incorrect to assume that the same treatment is<br>always given until death.<br>In clinical practice, for those people with CML, and who cannot be treated with dasatinib and<br>nilotinib, the treatment options are other TKIs, various combination of treatments, and finally<br>(only if they are suitable), BMSCT.<br>These post-failure treatments are likely to attract high costs (such as treating Graft versus<br>Host Disease; managing co-morbidities and serious adverse effects; hospitalisation; blood<br>tests; weekly visits to the GP; blood transfusion; hospital based out-patient visits etc). All<br>these costs, not only the initial drug or transplantation costs, should be taken into account. It is<br>too simplistic to conclude that only treatments such as dasatinib and nilotinib will result in high<br>treatment costs for every year of life lived with CML. Should dasatinib and nilotinib be<br>unavailable, the alternative treatments will result in even higher treatment costs.|Comment noted. This section has been<br>amended (accordingly, for clarity). See FAD<br>section 4.3.6|



1. Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 17 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|BMS|_Paragraph 4.3.8: „The Committee also considered that if each year of life were adjusted by_<br>_quality of life the resulting costs per QALY would be likely to be higher than the annual costs_<br>_of the drugs. The Committee agreed that these considerations would be important in checking_<br>_the face validity of each of the economic models and outputs……‟_<br>**Comments**: The argument above is intuitive and does seem to have „face value‟<br>If an intervention costs £1, and gives a year of life, the ICER would be £1 per life year gained.<br>If that year was given a utility weight (i.e. QALY weight - quality adjusted life years) of 0.5 the<br>ICER becomes £2 per QALY gained. However, BMS requests clarification of the Committee‟s<br>logic on this point, since a drug‟s acquisition costs are only one component of the total<br>treatment costs. It is important to recognise that any 3rd line intervention for CML includes a<br>combination of drugs, chemotherapeutic agents and BMSCT.<br>Thus, if a patient „fails‟ treatment more quickly, or has a poorer prognosis, on treatment A<br>compared with treatment B, it can be expected that treatment A will incur far greater third line<br>costs than treatment B. Consider the following case study. If most patients on<br>hydroxycarbamide will be classified as failures at 1 year, then a large proportion of them will<br>receive BMSCT. However, if a much smaller proportion of patients fail dasatinib at 1 year,<br>then much fewer patients will receive BMSCT. Therefore, the difference in front line<br>acquisition costs is offset.<br>In summary, in the presence of significant cost offsets, the TOTAL incremental lifetime cost<br>per patient can be a lot lower than the incremental lifetime acquisition costs.|This information was included in the ACD, but<br>has been amended following comments from<br>consultees.|



1. Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 18 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|BMS|_Paragraph 4.2.4: „The published data from the dasatinib trial were limited to 48 months of_<br>_follow-up and the manufacturer extrapolated longer-term progression-free survival_**_by_**<br>**_assuming that the monthly rate of progression after 48 months was equal to that_**<br>**_observed during the final year of the published data.’_**<br>_Paragraph 4.3.9: „the Committee noted that the transition probabilities were extrapolated_**_by_**<br>**_taking the rate of progression from months 36 to 48 forward_**_; the Committee was_<br>_concerned that this_**_implied no one would progress from the (healthy) complete_**<br>**_cytogenetic response state after 3 years_**_. It considered that this is not plausible, given that it_<br>_had heard from the clinical experts that a proportion of people with CML will experience_<br>_disease progression‟._<br>**Comments**: The highlighted parts of the above sentences are not a reasonable interpretation<br>of the evidence.<br>Firstly, in our economic model (Excel format), we actually extrapolated longer-term<br>progression-free survival based on the progression rate observed from months 6 and 48, not<br>the final year of the trial data (i.e. not by taking the rate of progression from month 36 and 48<br>forward).<br>Secondly, the AC suggests that BMS model extrapolation implies no one ever progressed<br>from the CCyR state after 4 years, this is incorrect.<br>Firstly, during the trial period, there is not progression observed in the trial from month 23 to<br>month 47 for patients who achieved CCyR (Table 1 [not reproduced here]).<br>Secondly, while there is no progression at the last year in the trial (see Table 1), we have not<br>used this information to inform the extrapolation process. Instead, earlier data was used to<br>derive the progression rates (i.e. data from months 6 and 48) and hence there is increasing<br>progression at 48 months onwards, especially with CCyR patients. Sample proportions from<br>the model are reproduced below to support this statement (**Error! Reference source not**<br>**found.**2 [not reproduced here]).|This information was included in the ACD, but<br>has been amended following comments from<br>consultees.|



1. Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 19 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|BMS|_Paragraph 4.2.4: „The rates of progression with the other treatments were taken from other_<br>_clinical studies and assumptions‟_<br>**Comments**: The above sentence is not a reasonable interpretation of the evidence.<br>The rates of progression were based on the initial best response levels. For example, patients<br>with CCyR would have a certain rate of progression, whilst patients do not respond to<br>treatment would have another rate of progression. In this case, the relationship between<br>progression and response levels are the same for all treatments. This relationship (rates of<br>progression) is based on dasatinib 4-year follow up trial data. Therefore, the rates of<br>progression with the other treatments were the same as those of dasatinib. However, since<br>the initial best response levels vary of different treatments, the progression free survival<br>become different.|This information was included in the ACD, but<br>has been amended following comments from<br>consultees.|
|BMS|_Paragraph 4.3.9: „First, the (BMS) model estimated that the cost for people receiving_<br>_interferon alfa was higher (in some cases double) than that of all of the other economic_<br>_models…….‟_<br>**Comments**: As noted in the summary of product characteristics for Interferon alfa-2a (Roferon<br>A): “Roferon-A should be administered under the supervision of a qualified physician<br>experienced in the management of the respective indication”<br>As such, the total monthly cost must include not only the cost of treatment, but also all<br>outpatient/ administration costs relating to treatment administration. Assuming that treatment<br>is needed three times per week, this would result in 13 outpatient visits per month (3 per week<br>* 4.33 weeks per month). Thus, administration costs would range from £325 per month (if all<br>were performed via a GP nurse at the practice) to £1,400 per month (if all were performed via<br>a hospital based oncology outpatient visit).<br>In terms of drug costs, assuming a body surface area of 1.7m2, and a dose of 6 MIU, the<br>monthly cost of treatment (including inevitable wastage due to lack of vial sharing) is £550 if<br>1ml vials are used, and £1,376 if 2.5ml vials are used (vial dose 10MU/ml).<br>Therefore BMS would argue that the cost of interferon used in our model is likely to be at the<br>lower end of the costs range, and as a result all ICERs represent values close to or on the<br>upper threshold for this comparison.|Comment noted. The Committee considered all<br>the evidence submitted, including evidence from<br>clinical trials, patient and clinical experts, the<br>Assessment Group‟s economic analysis and the<br>manufacturers‟ submissions. It also carefully<br>considered the comments received from<br>consultees and commentators in response to the<br>Assessment Report. See FAD section 4.3.14|

However, the Committee noted that_**_the SHTAC_**<br>**_Assessment Group’s model also had the major limitation of PenTAG’s model of_**<br>**_assessing treatment duration and overall survival by unrelated methods’_**_._<br>**Comments:**As BMS have noted consistently throughout all the appraisals of 2nd line CML<br>interventions, the simple, fundamental rule of any health economic model is that it is an<br>accurate representation of the underlying disease process. We are pleased that some of the<br>inaccuracies in the model have been acknowledged by the AC. However, the „PenTAG model<br>does not link treatment duration and overall survival‟ is the consequence of a fundamental<br>flaw in the model structure, that is, the response rate is not used as a common surrogate<br>outcome measure for OS and PFS. It is important to note that by changing the „treatment<br>duration‟ alone will not improve or correct the model. Indeed, BMS are deeply concerned that<br>no effort has been made to change the flawed modelling approach. As noted by the AC, the<br>base-case of SHTAC analysis is based a revised version of the PenTAG model, and it did not<br>fix the fundamental problem, but merely altered some data outputs (for example, PFS). It<br>would therefore seem perverse of NICE to make a reimbursement decision for a whole class<br>of interventions based on a flawed economic model.<br>The model initially developed by PenTAG, and slightly modified by SHTAC, have not<br>addressed this fundamental issue. Serious and fundamental flaws exist in both the<br>assumptions on which the model is built and the methods used in the construction. In<br>particular, the model allows for individuals to spend longer in one health state than they do<br>alive, and uses inconsistent approaches to modelling key parameters for different drugs<br>(implicitly stating that they act in a biologically different manner). The approach also allows for<br>individuals on the older, less effective, interventions to have a lower rate of disease<br>progression than those on newer and more effective drugs.|The Committee understood that the model<br>updated by SHTAC attempted to correct<br>PenTAG‟s overestimate of survival on<br>interferon alfa and the discrepancy between the<br>nilotinib and dasatinib treatment durations, but<br>the SHTAC base-case treatment durations still<br>did not reflect the fact that in clinical practice,<br>people will receive treatment until progression or<br>death (this was confirmed by the clinical<br>specialists; see section 4.3.6). See FAD section<br>4.3.18.<br>The Committee did not consider that a plausible<br>ICER had been presented in any of the<br>economic models, but agreed that the least<br>implausible analysis was the SHTAC scenario in<br>which the treatment durations of dasatinib, high-<br>dose imatinib and nilotinib were set to 10 years<br>with overall survival estimates of 12.4–<br>13.4 years. See FAD section 4.3.19|

|BMS|**Implications of not using a common surrogate outcome measure for OS and PFS**<br>The lack of link between response rates and PFS means that altering the value for the chosen<br>surrogate marker (Major Cytogenetic response – MCyR) has no impact on time in Progression<br>Free Survival (PFS). In other words, regardless of whether the MCyR response rate is set to<br>0% or 100%, the amount of time spent on therapy in chronic phase („CP on-treatment‟)<br>remains constant (Table 5 [not reproduced here]).|The Assessment relies on the available evidence<br>submitted to the Institute and that retrieved from<br>the published literature by the assessment<br>group.<br>The Committee considered all the evidence<br>submitted, including evidence from clinical trials,<br>patient and clinical experts, the Assessment<br>Group‟s economic analysis and the<br>manufacturers‟ submissions. It also carefully<br>considered the comments received from<br>consultees and commentators in response to the<br>Assessment Report.|
|BMS|In the model, individuals are also assumed to spend time in chronic phase without any<br>treatments („CP off-treatment‟), and the time spent in „CP off-treatment‟ is set as the difference<br>between Overall Survival (OS) and the amount of time spent in „CP on-treatment‟. As a result<br>of this approach, even when a patient does not respond to the treatment (response rate is set<br>to 0%), he/she can still remain in chronic phase without receiving any treatment – for as long<br>as 6 years! This is not supported by clinical data and fundamentally undermines the clinical<br>credibility of the model.<br>As noted by PenTAG in their original report, there is a body of evidence supporting the<br>hypothesis that a complete cytogenic response (CCyR) (defined as no Ph+ chromosomes in<br>the bone marrow) is a predictor of both OS and PFS (Figure 1 [not reproduced here]).<br>It is important to note that (a) both plots were derived from individuals in the same clinical trial<br>(IRIS) and (b) other authors have identified the same relationship (Druker et al 2006, Roy et al<br>2006).|The Assessment relies on the available evidence<br>submitted to the Institute and that retrieved from<br>the published literature by the assessment<br>group.<br>The Committee considered all the evidence<br>submitted, including evidence from clinical trials,<br>patient and clinical experts, the Assessment<br>Group‟s economic analysis and the<br>manufacturers‟ submissions. It also carefully<br>considered the comments received from<br>consultees and commentators in response to the<br>Assessment Report.|



1. Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 24 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|BMS|BMS consider it extremely unlikely, given the proven causal link between CCyR and PFS, that<br>no such link exists between MCyR (defined as ≤35% Ph+ chromosomes in the bone marrow).<br>The PenTAG/SHTAC model is therefore fundamentally flawed in the way it represents the<br>underlying disease, and as such the model cannot form the basis of rational decision making.<br>In addition to the problem of „no link between response rates and PFS‟, another issue with<br>PenTAG/SHTAC model is the counter-intuitive and inconsistent methods used to model OS<br>and PFS of different treatments. Given the Committee‟s desire to include face validity checks<br>into the decision making process it is worthwhile reflecting, in abstract terms, on the nature of<br>CML. In general, for people who do not have CML, the rate of death will increase over time by<br>virtue of the natural aging process. For people with CML, because of natural disease<br>progression, loss of response, intolerance to treatment, time, etc, the rate of death would also<br>be expected to increase over time. This leads to the conclusion that an increasing rate of<br>death should be observed in any parametric function used to model OS and PFS.<br>The PenTAG approach used to model OS is based on a Weibull model with a gamma value<br>(the parameter controlling the rate of change) being set to less than one. This means that the<br>rate of death decreases over time.<br>In terms of PFS, with the exception of high dose imatinib (HDI) where a Weibull function is<br>used (and the fitted progression rate increases over time), all interventions are modelled using<br>exponential distributions. This approach is flawed on three levels:<br>1. It assumes a constant rate of disease progression over time. That is, if a patient has had<br>CML for 20 years, the rate of progression is the same as if the patient has had it for one<br>day.<br>2. By using a different method to model high dose imatinib (HDI) and IFN, the result is that<br>after a short period of time (from model cycle 10 onwards), patients on HDI have a higher<br>rate of progression than those on interferon (IFN).<br>3. As a direct consequence of this mixed modelling approach, individuals in the IFN arm can<br>expect to spend longer in the PFS state than alive (predicted undiscounted  PFS state<br>occupancy: 3.47 years, predicted overall survival: 3.41 years), see Table 6 [not reproduced<br>here].|The Assessment relies on the available evidence<br>submitted to the Institute and that retrieved from<br>the published literature by the assessment<br>group.<br>The Committee considered all the evidence<br>submitted, including evidence from clinical trials,<br>patient and clinical experts, the Assessment<br>Group‟s economic analysis and the<br>manufacturers‟ submissions. It also carefully<br>considered the comments received from<br>consultees and commentators in response to the<br>Assessment Report.|

It noted, however,_<br>_that two main scenario analyses as well as a sensitivity analysis of survival versus treatment_<br>_duration had been developed to reflect this problem.‟_<br>**Comments**: BMS notes that, in order to address concerns relating to the implausibly low<br>treatment durations for all TKI‟s, additional scenario analyses were performed by the SHTAC<br>where these values were fixed to either 6.5 or 10 years.<br>BMS would like to draw attention to the fact that „treatment duration‟ is a model output and<br>NOT a model input. As such, it cannot be directly altered. By altering treatment durations, it<br>does not make the model more reflective of clinical practice and underlying disease, In order<br>to demonstrate this, we have replicated the analyses using the information provided by the<br>SHTAC group on 18th May 2011. Specifically, the SHTAC informed us that the additional<br>scenario analyses were based on changing lambda values for PFS on the „treatment duration‟<br>worksheet (cells BC12, BC14, BC16). When we used the SHTAC‟s method, and replicated<br>the PFS and OS, it shows that in all cases PFS is higher than OS for a period of time (Figure<br>2 [not reproduced here]). BMS believe the method used to overcome the original issue causes<br>more problems than it solves. Intuitively, in any oncology model, one of the key face validity<br>tests is that there can never be more patients in the progression-free health state than there<br>are currently alive (since this would result in a negative number of patients the post-<br>progression health state). When the predicted OS and PFS curves for each intervention are<br>plotted for an assumed treatment duration at 10 years, it is clear that this fundamental<br>principle is violated.<br>BMS would like to emphasise that this is not a minor issue. For patients on high dose imatinib<br>(HDI), the number of individuals predicted to be in PFS is higher than those in OS for over 7<br>years. The corresponding values for nilotinib and dasatinib are 3.2 years and 1.2 years<br>respectively.<br>Once again, this suggests that the model analyses are fundamentally flawed, and as such it<br>would be perverse of the Committee to use the model as the basis for rational decision<br>making.|The Assessment relies on the available evidence<br>submitted to the Institute and that retrieved from<br>the published literature by the assessment<br>group.<br>The Committee considered all the evidence<br>submitted, including evidence from clinical trials,<br>patient and clinical experts, the Assessment<br>Group‟s economic analysis and the<br>manufacturers‟ submissions. It also carefully<br>considered the comments received from<br>consultees and commentators in response to the<br>Assessment Report.|

|BMS|_Paragraph 4.3.16: „However, the Committee agreed that_**_the available evidence on life_**<br>**_extension was too weak and was not considered to be robust_**_. In addition, no data were_<br>_presented for the interventions as used in clinical practice. The Committee concluded that_<br>_dasatinib, high-dose imatinib and nilotinib do not fulfil the end-of-life criteria for people with_<br>_CML in the accelerated and blast-crisis phases.‟_<br>**Comments**: The NICE guidance for „Appraising life-extending, end-of-life treatments‟ states<br>that end-of-life treatments must be „robust and can be shown or reasonably inferred from<br>either progression free survival or overall survival (taking account of trials in which cross-over<br>has occurred and been accounted for in the effectiveness review).‟ Even in the absence of<br>comparative data for accelerated and blast phase disease, it is difficult to appreciate why the<br>AC considers a clinical trial with 2 year follow-up data is not robust (Apperley et al 2009,<br>Kantarjian et al 2009, Cortes et al 2008). Since achieving and maintaining of response have<br>been proven to be reliable predictors of longer term survival benefits (Marin et al 2008), the<br>data were considered robust enough to form the basis of a decision by the European<br>Commission to approve dasatinib in advanced CML following a positive opinion from the<br>European Medicines Agency.<br>Indeed, on this basis, one must query how many orphan and ultra-orphan medicines are likely<br>to satisfy the AC‟s (unspecified) standards of robustness. Following the Servier case (Servier<br>Laboratories Limited v National Institute for Health and Clinical Excellence (2010)) it is clear<br>that NICE must place sufficient weight on evidence that is central to a party‟s case, particularly<br>when such evidence has been held as being robust enough for marketing authorisation<br>purposes. We also query whether the Institute‟s appraisal procedures are adequate in<br>assessing the clinical and cost-effectiveness of ultra-orphan drugs.|The Committee noted that in the advanced<br>stages of CML, life expectancy is generally less<br>than 24 months. The Committee also agreed that<br>this is a very small population, because fewer<br>than 10% of all people with CML will present at<br>this stage. However, the Committee agreed that<br>the available evidence on life extension was too<br>weak and was not considered to be robust. In<br>addition, no data were presented for the<br>interventions as used in clinical practice. See<br>FAD section 4.3.29|

|BMS|_Paragraph 4: „As a result of comments received during consultation,_**_it was agreed to_**<br>**_combine an appraisal of the three technologies, dasatinib, high-dose imatinib (600 mg_**<br>**_and 800 mg) and nilotinib_**_, to establish their comparative incremental clinical effectiveness_<br>_and cost effectiveness. The following actions were implemented‟_<br>**Comments:**BMS has serious concerns about the procedures which were followed, and<br>believes these procedures to be unfair, perverse, and outside the scope of NICE‟s jurisdiction.<br>These concerns can be summarised as follows:<br>In place of HDI as a comparator, the AC chose to use hydroxycarbamide, despite it<br>representing obsolete clinical practice, as made clear by clinical expert submissions during the<br>course of this appraisal. In doing so, the AC has shifted its review from an area where there is<br>robust - albeit limited - evidence (i.e., a randomised trial of dasatinib compared with HDI) to an<br>area for which there is, inevitably, almost no evidence and where evidence is unlikely ever to<br>arise. Any attempt to set up clinical trials in order to generate data comparing dasatinib or<br>nilotinib to hydroxycarbamide would be unethical. This is because the Declaration of Helsinki<br>requires that clinical research involving any „new intervention‟ must be conducted against the<br>„best current proven intervention‟ and that the use of other interventions, placebo or no<br>therapy is only possible where „no current proven intervention exists‟. The 1996 version of the<br>Declaration, which applies to all pharmaceutical clinical research within the EU, provides that<br>„[i]n any medical study, every patient - including those of a control group, if any - should be<br>assured of the best proven diagnostic and therapeutic method. This does not exclude the use<br>of inert placebo in studies where no proven diagnostic or therapeutic method exists.‟ These<br>issues are particularly relevant in the oncology space. By selecting hydroxycarbamide as the<br>most appropriate comparator, the AC is moving from a position where it has meaningful<br>comparative data to a position that is likely to remain evidence free.|The Assessment relies on the available evidence<br>submitted to the Institute and that retrieved from<br>the published literature by the assessment<br>group.<br>The clinical specialists suggested that if<br>dasatinib, high-dose imatinib or nilotinib were not<br>available, people would receive treatment with<br>interferon alfa, hydroxycarbamide or best<br>supportive care, and that for many people<br>hydroxycarbamide or interferon alfa are<br>considered to be little better than best supportive<br>care. It also heard that bone marrow stem cell<br>transplantation could be used, although it carries<br>high risks and is restricted to fit, younger people.<br>See FAD section 4.3.3|
|BMS|The AC states that „it was agreed…‟. To be clear, BMS did not “agree” to this. In our previous<br>responses to the TAR and the ACD, we have pointed out that HDI should be the comparator,<br>and not be grouped together with dasatinib and nilotinib as interventions. The underlying basis<br>for this decision is unclear and lacks transparency.|This information was included in the ACD, but<br>has been amended following comments from<br>consultees.|

Treatment with nilotinib will therefore continue for 15 years.<br>60% of patients will be non-responders whose treatment will be withdrawn after 1 year.<br>Average treatment duration will therefore be 6.6 years (40% of 15 years (6) plus 60% of 1<br>year (0.6)).<br>The results of a study conducted in Scotland3support the view that CML patients will not stay<br>on treatment for as long as 10 years as the Appraisal Committee has concluded. The study<br>collected data on tolerability and clinical outcome of patients treated with second generation<br>TKI following discontinuation of imatinib therapy because of adverse events or failed<br>response. |Novartis|**5.2   No link between PFS and major cytogenetic response (MCyR)**<br>The SHTAC model did not address the fundamental concern that the MCyR rates were not<br>linked to progression-free survival (PFS) in the model. PFS is used to estimate the treatment<br>duration in the model and the evidence shows that a higher response is associated with better<br>PFS which in turn leads to better survival. It seems reasonable to link PFS with MCyR<br>because PFS reflects both the level of response and is also used to estimate the treatment<br>duration. Instead, the current model structure assumes that MCyR is linked to OS and not<br>PFS. This approach by SHTAC leads to an underestimate of the benefits of nilotinib in this<br>setting. Novartis cannot address this limitation because it requires a structural change to the<br>AG model.|The Committee noted that the PenTAG model<br>did not link treatment duration with overall<br>survival and that some of the results were not<br>plausible. In particular, it noted that the<br>estimated overall survival for interferon alfa was<br>implausible and the treatment duration for people<br>receiving nilotinib was lower than would be seen<br>in clinical practice, given the estimated overall<br>survival. See FAD section 4.3.17<br>The Committee understood that the model<br>updated by SHTAC attempted to correct<br>PenTAG‟s overestimate of survival on<br>interferon alfa and the discrepancy between the<br>nilotinib and dasatinib treatment durations, but<br>the SHTAC base-case treatment durations still<br>did not reflect the fact that in clinical practice,<br>people will receive treatment until progression or<br>death (this was confirmed by the clinical<br>specialists; see section 4.3.6).See FAD section<br>4.3.18|

|Novartis|**5.3  The treatment duration for nilotinib and dasatinib of 10 years is not supported by**<br>**any data**<br>The initial analysis that was conducted by both PenTAG and SHTAC on treatment duration<br>was based on PFS data and discontinuation rates from the pivotal trials of both nilotinib and<br>dasatinib. These data showed that dasatinib had lower discontinuation rates when compared<br>with nilotinib resulting in higher treatments costs for the former when the discontinuation rates<br>were applied in the model. In the updated SHTAC analysis, the lambda values for PFS have<br>been adjusted to reflect 6.5 years and 10 years treatment durations. However the predicted<br>PFS curves are not extrapolations based on any data as is expected in economic modelling.<br>To illustrate this point Novartis has extracted the PFS curves from the AG model. Figure 1 [not<br>reproduced here]shows the fitted PFS for the original SHTAC base case analysis. Figure 2<br>shows the PFS curves estimating the most optimistic assumption of 10 year treatment<br>duration compared with trial data.<br>As can be seen in Figure 1 [not reproduced here] above, the original SHTAC base case<br>analysis is based on extrapolations from the data.  However the 10 years treatment duration<br>PFS is not based on any fit to the data as can be seen in Figure 2. This is a limitation in the<br>analysis because the type of curve fitted is determined by the type of data available. In the<br>absence of data as in Figure 2 [not reproduced here], it is hard to justify such a PFS fit in the<br>model. This issue is compounded further by the fact that the model does not link the PFS to<br>OS as discussed earlier. All this adds to the uncertainty in the model and it is likely that the<br>model is underestimating the benefits of nilotinib by not linking PFS to OS.|The Committee heard from the clinical<br>specialists that in more than 50% of people with<br>imatinib-resistant CML treated with dasatinib or<br>nilotinib, there is a good response to treatment<br>and that this response is usually as good as the<br>initial response to standard-dose imatinib. The<br>clinical specialists expected that these people<br>would receive dasatinib or nilotinib treatment for<br>the rest of their lives, and possibly have a nearly<br>normal life expectancy (that is, at least 10 more<br>years).See FAD section 4.3.6.<br>The Committee did not consider that a plausible<br>ICER had been presented in any of the<br>economic models, but agreed that the least<br>implausible analysis was the SHTAC scenario in<br>which the treatment durations of dasatinib, high-<br>dose imatinib and nilotinib were set to 10 years<br>with overall survival estimates of 12.4–<br>13.4 years. See FAD section 4.3.19.<br>The Committee agreed that treatment duration<br>could be less than 10 years but the estimate of<br>6.5 years, which was based on treatment being<br>withdrawn in all people who did not have a<br>complete cytogenetic response, was not<br>plausible. See FAD section 4.3.22|

|Novartis|**5.4  The survival benefit for nilotinib assumed in the model should be the same as that**<br>**of dasatinib**<br>Although the Committee accepts clinical opinion that nilotinib and dasatinib are broadly the<br>same with respect to their benefits, they have assumed a longer overall survival and hence a<br>higher QALY gain for dasatinib based on the dasatinib MCyR. If the treatment duration is<br>assumed to be the same (an unsubstantiated assumption) then it is logical to also assume the<br>same survival benefit for the two drugs. In the base case SHTAC analysis, dasatinib‟s<br>treatment duration was based on data from the pivotal dasatinib trial and this also translated<br>into a higher benefit through the MCyR. In the additional scenario analysis, the Committee has<br>assumed equal treatment durations based on no data, suggesting that the survival benefit<br>should also be assumed to be the same (in the absence of data). Novartis views the approach<br>by the Committee to be unfair and suggests that the same survival gain for nilotinib and<br>dasatinib is assumed.<br>However the above not withstanding, if survival for nilotinib is to be based on observed MCyR<br>rates from the pivotal trials, then the most up to date data should be used i.e. a MCyR of 56%<br>for nilotinib. This estimate was noted in the SHTAC AR but was not applied in either the<br>SHTAC‟s base case or updated scenario analyses. When this rate is applied in the model, the<br>life years gained are 13.25 compared to life years gained of 13.40 for dasatinib. This confirms<br>our view (consistent with that of clinical experts and the Appraisal Committee) that the efficacy<br>of the two drugs should be treated as the same.<br>To ensure fairness, Novartis suggests that the same life years, and hence same QALYs<br>gained, for the two drugs be used in their considerations. With this in mind, the conclusion in<br>paragraph 4.3.12 of the ACD that dasatinib dominates nilotinib is incorrect. Novartis has<br>conducted updated analysis assuming the same survival gain as dasatinib (see appendix 1<br>[not reproduced here]).|The Committee considered the additional<br>analyses submitted by Novartis, including the the<br>assumption of survival benefit equal to that of<br>dasatinib, see FAD 4.3.22<br>The Committee also agreed that there was no<br>good evidence to distinguish between dasatinib<br>and nilotinib; a conclusion also supported by the<br>clinical specialists. See FAD section 4.3.9.|

|Novartis|**5.5  Utility of HU overestimated**<br>The utility of HU in the chronic phase (CP) has been assumed to be the same as that of the<br>second generation TKIs - that is 0.85. Clinical opinion (as discussed earlier) suggested that<br>HU does not lead to any improvement in either PFS or OS. For HU to have the same utility as<br>nilotinib which was specifically designed to delay disease progression and therefore lead to<br>better survival is perverse and overestimates the benefit of HU in this setting. It is significant<br>that Professor Clark has suggested that patients with chronic phase CML treated with nilotinib<br>have a near normal quality of life.<br>Patients who receive SCT, a potentially curative treatment option, have a utility of 0.71 in the<br>model, implying that a patient on HU will do better than a patient who has undergone a<br>successful transplant. This is both counterintuitive and inaccurate and is a clear indication that<br>the utility benefits of HU have been exaggerated. There are no published utility values for HU<br>in this setting but given the limited benefits HU confers on patients based on clinical expert<br>opinion, it could be argued that the utility of HU in CP could be between the utility of SCT and<br>that of nilotinib. Novartis has therefore decided to consider the average of utility of SCT and<br>nilotinib. This gives an average utility of 0.78 and this value is applied in the updated analysis<br>(see appendix 1 [not reproduced here]).|The Committee did not agree with Novartis that<br>the utility value for people treated with<br>hydroxycarbamide should be lower for the same<br>health states achieved by other treatments. It<br>accepted that health state durations were shorter<br>with hydroxycarbamide but thought that this<br>should not be compounded by utility value<br>adjustments.  See FAD section 4.3.22.|

The clinical experts at the advisory board suggested that in<br>the absence of any data on the use of HU in the second line setting, the only evidence (albeit<br>with many limitations) will be the first line HU data from the various published trials of patients<br>with a high Sokal risk score. The clinical experts explained that 5 year OS for HU in the<br>second line will be expected to be less than 10% given that HU does not induce CCyR, a<br>marker for improved survival. Novartis calculated a 5 year survival on HU of 16% based on<br>first line data. It should be noted that this survival is an overestimate because this is from the<br>first line setting for patients who are not resistant to imatinib. Given that clinical experts<br>suggested that the 5 year OS of patients on HU will be less than 10%, the use of the 16%<br>survival in the Novartis model was an overestimate of the OS of patients on in the CP.<br>Although this survival estimate for HU was used in the model, Novartis noted that the results<br>were to be treated with caution because of the limitations discussed earlier. Novartis therefore<br>believes that the survival of patients on HU has been overestimated in the SHTAC model<br>resulting in a reduced incremental survival benefit for nilotinib.<br>Novartis has therefore conducted updated scenario analyses in the AG model to address this<br>concern by assuming that the OS for HU was 3 years (See appendix 1 [not reproduced here]).|The Committee considered the additional<br>analyses submitted by Novartis including the<br>lower estimate of survival with<br>hydroxycarbamide. See FAD sections 4.3.22.and<br>4.3.23<br>The Committee accepted that health state<br>durations were shorter with hydroxycarbamide<br>but thought that this should not be compounded<br>by utility value adjustments. ## Confidential until publication 

|**Nominating**<br>**organisation **|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|CML Support<br>Group UK|There is a more general criticism applicable here concerning the particular genetic<br>mutations for which a TKI is active against. The principle underlining a TKI is that it is<br>effective only against those mutations for which it was designed and that it is counter<br>intuitive and counter productive to deploy it when they are not present.<br>We argue that, with likely recipient minute patient populations for what are already rare<br>diseases, RCTs, as currently designed, should be abandoned altogether and substituted<br>with a more appropriate design based on a Bayesian approach.<br>Bayesian approaches imply updating prior probability distributions of efficacy through new<br>data in order to give rise to posterior probability distributions.<br>There is a requirement for sufficient information to be available to empower the statistics<br>required which would be sourced from clinical practice and biological evidence, other<br>clinical studies, the natural history of the disease, analogies with more frequently<br>occurring diseases etc.<br>The ongoing development of digitalized e-health records with common design matrixes<br>accumulated in instantly accessible data warehouses offer tantalizing possibilities in terms<br>of the contribution they could make to such an approach.|Comment noted.|

|---|---|---|
|CML Support<br>Group UK|**Innovation status: criteria for qualification**<br>The Committee does not find nilotinib and dasatinib to be innovative_“in terms of_<br>_pharmacological progress beyond imatinib”_<br>Imatinib is, however, a drug that does attract their innovation classification.  They give no<br>substance to this decision.<br>There are, as far as we are aware, no publicly available NICE criteria deployed to grade<br>the degree of innovation displayed in any particular case and it seems such classificatory<br>work proceeds on an ad hoc basis.<br>Of course we accept that imatinib represented a 'step change' in cancer therapy in much<br>the same way as penicillin represented a 'step change', as in a first of its kind, but would<br>think it either perverse or demonstrative of a misunderstanding of the science not to<br>regard successor generations as representing an innovation.<br>Pharmacogenomics, the tailoring of products to particular genetic configurations<br>characteristic of small sub sets of patient populations for diseases like CML, sits at the<br>leading edge of advanced manufacturing industries like pharmaceuticals.<br>Scientists working in research institutions and those engaged in similar work in academic<br>institutions, would be surprised, to say the least, that their work in developing successor<br>generations of inhibitors is not considered to represent_“pharmacological progress beyond_<br>_imatinib”_and would no doubt wish the committee, or indeed NICE, to articulate their<br>innovation policy in rather more detail than hitherto has been the case, in order to<br>demonstrate that they had not made any_“pharmacological progress beyond imatinib”_.|The Committee considered that the development<br>of dasatinib and nilotinib was not a further major<br>innovation. The Committee did not identify any<br>potential significant and substantial health-related<br>benefits that had not been included in the<br>economic models. See FAD section 4.3.30|
|CML Support<br>Group UK|Elsewhere in the appraisal the committee accepts, and indeed use as a resource in their<br>appraisal work, evidence supplied by expert clinicians that confirms that, in more than<br>50% of the patients involved, a_“good response”_to nilotinib and dasatinib was achieved<br>with patients able to anticipate living_“possibly with a near to normal life expectancy (that_<br>_is, more than 10 years)”_.  They also note responses were_“usually as good”_as the<br>response to the first line drug they accept to be innovative, standard dose imatinib.<br>It is axiomatic that the_“more than 50%"_of patients referred to above would certainly<br>regard dasatinib or nilotinb as representing_“pharmacological progress beyond imatinib”_<br>especially since the alternative would be HU.|Comment noted.|

|CML Support<br>Group UK|**Equalities**<br>There are  issues to be considered that fall under this heading. One are ethnic minorities, especially the<br>African Caribbean community, and their gross under-representation in donor registries<br>and the other is the population on the upper side of the current median age for stem cell<br>transplantation.<br>We would like it noted that in the former case greater encouragement ought to be offered<br>to organisations like the African Carribbean Leukaemia Trust to assist them in building<br>ethnically diverse donor registry and secondly, that additional resources should be<br>devoted to the research of new technologies that involve the use of inhibitors in stem cell<br>transplants with the objective of making them safer and more easily tolerated than is the<br>case at present.|The Committee noted the argument that if<br>dasatinib, high-dose imatinib or nilotinib are not<br>recommended for the treatment of imatinib-<br>resistant CML and that this could raise issues in<br>relation to race, age (the elderly), and<br>comorbidities. |**Commentator **||**Comment**|**Response**|
